<a id="am-552"></a>
### Amendment 552

*Proposal for a regulation*

**Article 196 – paragraph 10**

| Text proposed by the Commission | Amendment |
| --- | --- |
| ***10. At the request of the competent authority designated pursuant to the Cybersecurity Act which is to replace Regulation (EU) 2019/881 of the European Parliament and of the Council after non-compliance remedial measures under the Cybersecurity Act have been exhausted, the national regulatory authority where networks and services are provided shall withdraw the right to provide networks and services under general authorisation or individual rights of use of radio spectrum pursuant to Article 9(4), point d) and Article 20 in cases where the provider is not complying with the ICT supply chain security requirements of the Cybersecurity Act.*** | ***deleted*** |

*Justification*

Many elements in this provision (legal act, measure, authority designated) are non existant, at least yet. NRAs have general obligations within article 196 to supervise compliance with general authorisation obligations referred to article 9(4) and also article 20. And there is also a process well described in particular in 196(4). Therefore this provision is not necessary.

### Amendment 561

*Proposal for a regulation*

**Article 206 c (new)**

*Regulation (EU) 2022/612*

Article 11 – paragraph 1

| Present text | Amendment |
| --- | --- |
|  | ***Article 206c*** |
|  | ***paragraph 1 is replaced by the following:*** |
| The average wholesale charge that the visited network operator may levy on the roaming provider for the provision of regulated data roaming services by means of the visited network shall not exceed a safeguard limit of EUR ***2,00*** per gigabyte of data transmitted. That maximum wholesale charge shall decrease to EUR ***1,80*** per gigabyte of data transmitted on 1 January ***2023***, to EUR ***1,55*** per gigabyte on 1 January ***2024, to EUR 1,30 per gigabyte on 1 January 2025, to EUR 1,10 per gigabyte on 1 January 2026 and to EUR 1,00 per gigabyte by 1 January 2027***, after which, without prejudice to Article 21, it shall remain at EUR ***1,00*** per gigabyte of data transmitted until 30 June ***2032***. | "The average wholesale charge that the visited network operator may levy on the roaming provider for the provision of regulated data roaming services by means of the visited network shall not exceed a safeguard limit of EUR ***0,65*** per gigabyte of data transmitted. That maximum wholesale charge shall decrease to EUR ***0,50*** per gigabyte of data transmitted on 1 January ***2030***, to EUR ***0,40*** per gigabyte on 1 January ***2031***, after which, without prejudice to Article 21, it shall remain at EUR ***0,40*** per gigabyte of data transmitted until 30 June ***2037***. |
|  | ***"*** |

(32022R0612)

*Justification*

The wholesale caps set by Regulation (EU) 2022/612 no longer reflect market reality. According to BEREC data, the average EEA wholesale data roaming price was EUR 0.57/GB in Q3 2025, less than half of the EUR 1.30/GB cap then in force and well above the lowest prices paid and charged, which ranged between EUR 0.03 and 0.04/GB in the same period. The cap nevertheless remains decisive for operators with weak bargaining power: BEREC observes that MVNO wholesale rates tend to remain close to the regulated caps. Following the approach of the ITRE report of 14 October 2021, which called for significantly lower wholesale caps than those finally adopted, this amendment brings the caps in line with market developments. The gradual glide path to 2030 gives operators time to adapt, as unit costs continue to fall with growing traffic volumes. Spectrum is a scarce resource held by a few mobile network operators in each Member State. Other providers can compete only by using someone else’s network: domestic MVNOs through wholesale access, and operators from other Member States through roaming. Bringing wholesale roaming charges closer to domestic wholesale access prices puts both on a more equal footing. It allows providers to compete for customers across borders and creates competitive pressure in national markets where retail prices are high, strengthening the single market. Consumers benefit directly. The wholesale cap limits the surcharge that providers may apply when consumption abroad exceeds the fair-use limit. It is also the reference for calculating the minimum data volume that consumers with open data bundles can use when roaming. Lower caps therefore mean lower charges and larger roaming data allowances for frequent travellers, cross-border commuters, students and business users. They also allow smaller operators and MVNOs to offer “roam like at home” sustainably.

<a id="explanatory-statement"></a>
## EXPLANATORY STATEMENT

I. Introduction and summary

For more than two decades, from the 2002 regulatory package to the 2018 European Electronic Communications Code, the Union has set the framework for electronic communications through directives. Each Member State transposed and applied them in its own way. The framework was European, but in practice the Union regulated 27 domestic markets. Telecom operators were regulated in detail, country by country, while the companies that increasingly shape how Europeans communicate, from messaging platforms and operating systems to device manufacturers and the largest content and cloud networks, operated on a global scale and were largely outside that framework. The consequences are well documented, most recently in the Draghi report: fragmented national markets, operators too small to invest at the scale needed, a persistent investment gap in fibre, 5G and edge computing, and growing dependence on non-European technology and gatekeepers.

Fragmentation is not the only reason why the framework must change. The networks themselves are changing. As the Commission’s 2024 White Paper How to master Europe’s digital infrastructure needs? showed, connectivity and computing are converging. Network functions are becoming virtualised, software-defined and cloud-native, and are moving to the edge. Cloud and content providers build and operate their own global networks, from content delivery networks to submarine cables. The “Connected Collaborative Computing” network envisaged by the White Paper blurs the line between those who provide connectivity and those who provide digital services. Electronic communications networks, cloud and edge infrastructure are becoming one continuum of digital networks, on which artificial intelligence, industrial applications and public services increasingly depend.

The same convergence is now taking place between space and terrestrial mobile networks. Direct-to-device technology allows ordinary smartphones to connect to satellites in low Earth orbit, using spectrum and standards shared with terrestrial networks. Since 2025, a global satellite operator has acquired large amounts of mobile spectrum to offer mobile satellite services directly to consumers. In May 2026, the Commission proposed an EU-level selection procedure for the 2 GHz mobile satellite band. Satellite and terrestrial mobile connectivity are becoming complementary layers of one network, competing for the same customers and the same scarce spectrum. But this convergence extends further to the whole digital ecosystem, in particular cloud, AI and computing. Geopolitics enters into play and impacts commercial strategies and choices. European players have to face numerous challenges that are global.

Therefore, this convergence calls for a different regulatory approach. Rules designed for separate, national and technology-specific networks cannot govern an integrated, cross-border ecosystem of digital networks. The Rapporteur therefore welcomes the Commission’s choice of a directly applicable regulation and believes that the Digital Networks Act (DNA) should become the framework for digital networks as a whole: technology-neutral, based on the same rules for the same services, and European.

The draft report sets out how to rebalance the ecosystem. It builds the DNA on three drivers of competitiveness: investment, simplification and harmonisation. It adds what Europe has learned it cannot do without: digital sovereignty, built on interoperability, a level playing field and “same service, same rules”, and resilience of the networks on which the economy, security and citizens depend. Last but not least, it puts citizens at the centre: easier access to emergency services, instant public warnings, full accessibility and better protection against fraud.

In short, the Rapporteur proposes to turn the DNA from a reform of telecom-sector rules into a European strategy for the whole connectivity ecosystem. Investment-friendly spectrum, fibre and access rules, simpler procedures and a genuine single market drive competitiveness. The same rules for the same services and interoperability underpin digital sovereignty. A European preparedness plan backed by investment, the defence of European airwaves and space assets, and emergency access that never fails make networks resilient. Every citizen gains: accessible services, an emergency number that works from any application and any place, instant warnings, cheaper roaming and better protection against fraud.

1. Digital sovereignty for competitiveness and resilience

The Rapporteur considers that Union must set the terms for the whole European digital connectivity ecosystem, not only for telecom operators.

***A general authorisation that covers the digital ecosystem***

In line with the principle of “same service, same rules” advocated in the Draghi report, the Rapporteur proposes that number-independent interpersonal communications services should be brought within the general authorisation regime and that access to emergency services via those services should become mandatory. Content delivery networks and submarine communication cables should be explicitly recognised as electronic communications networks. This would bring strategic infrastructure in Europe, much of it owned by non-European players, within the scope of European rules.

***A level playing field with the largest players***

On the relationship between network operators and the largest content and application providers, the Rapporteur proposes a targeted solution. Only undertakings that originate or deliver at least 2% of peak downstream traffic in the Union should be covered. They should be subject to an obligation to negotiate interconnection (as public network operators do with one another), backed by dispute resolution by national regulatory authorities. All other providers should be explicitly excluded. Smaller content providers, start-ups and end-users would not be affected, which addresses the net neutrality concerns raised in earlier debates on “fair share”.

Recognising that content delivery also depends on functions performed by actors other than internet service providers, the Rapporteur proposes to extend open internet obligations. Content and application providers, providers of artificial intelligence and operating systems, and content delivery networks should refrain from practices that compromise the equal treatment of traffic where they are able to influence traffic management or delivery quality. The Rapporteur also proposes to replace the voluntary conciliation mechanism proposed by the Commission, which was aimed at solving commercial disputes, with a standing facility for ecosystem cooperation, convened by the ODN. It would provide a forum for structured dialogue focused on constructive cooperation between network operators, content providers and device manufacturers, with an annual BEREC opinion on areas for this cooperation.

***Interoperability***

The Rapporteur proposes a new provision on operator-neutral terminal equipment, under which manufacturers should support standardised network functionalities for the customers of every operator authorised in the Union. BEREC should also review and, where appropriate, update its guidance on the network termination point, with a view to protecting end-users’ free choice of terminal equipment (“router freedom”) and preventing fragmentation of that market.

***Sovereignty in spectrum and space***

Given the convergence of satellite and terrestrial connectivity, the Rapporteur proposes that a portion of each newly harmonised band for wireless broadband could be awarded at Union level, through a selection procedure, to operators meeting strict resilience and sovereignty criteria. The selected operators would hold one authorisation valid throughout the Union and be subject to a wholesale access obligation. European ITU satellite filings should be jointly defended, with action against speculative or abusive filings and Commission approval for transfers linked to harmonised spectrum.

2. Competitiveness: investment, simplification and harmonisation

For the Rapporteur, competitiveness is not a slogan. It means more private investment in networks, less red tape for operators of every size, and one European market instead of 27. The Draghi report identified fragmentation, underinvestment and regulatory complexity as the core weaknesses of the European connectivity sector. The draft report answers with three mutually reinforcing levers. The Rapporteur proposes to extend the objectives of the Regulation to the competitiveness of the overall European economy and to add a new general principle - to avoid unnecessary regulatory, administrative or financial burdens on undertakings.

***2.1. Investment***

The Rapporteur aims to make spectrum an investment asset rather than a source of revenue for public budgets.

Spectrum rights. The Rapporteur supports the Commission’s proposal that rights of use for spectrum should, in principle, be granted for an unlimited duration. Their review, which the Commission proposes no earlier than 20 years after award, should be accompanied by a market analysis and take place every 15 years thereafter. Where a limited duration is chosen, the minimum should rise from 40 to 50 years, renewal should be automatic and for an indefinite period upon request, and any decision not to renew should be taken at least three years in advance.

Fees. The Rapporteur proposes that coverage and densification commitments should take priority over one-off payments. One-off fees should be allowed only where the authority demonstrates that they serve efficient use and do not hinder investment, and the total level of fees should never become a burden that delays deployment. The common European methodologies for annual fees and reserve prices, proposed by the Commission as recommendations, should become binding delegated acts, and the Commission should be able to set maximum fees or reserve prices where national application still discourages investment.

Award procedures. To give legal certainty, the Commission should be able to require the withdrawal of a national spectrum measure only in case of a serious violation, and it should identify and recommend the most pro-investment authorisation processes. Harmonised bands should be authorised within 18 months instead of 24 months as proposed by the Commission. A new densification principle should encourage small cells, with at least half of their cost offset through lower spectrum fees.

Fibre deployment, together with people. The Rapporteur supports the copper switch-off and the sustainability conditions proposed by the Commission, but replaces the fixed 2035 deadline with a clear, enforceable sequence that starts once those conditions are met. Regulators should identify switch-off areas within six months and publish, within 12 months, the areas that meet the sustainability conditions. Member States should then adopt a binding legal act within 12 months and require the switch-off to take place without undue delay. Nobody should be left behind: switch-off should require affordable services of at least comparable quality for all users relying on copper, and users should be able to request a fibre connection where such connectivity is present nearby.

Access rules. The Rapporteur supports the ladder of remedies proposed by the Commission and proposes to make access rules more symmetric, with a stronger role for commitments. Regulators should consider commitments offered by operators before imposing any remedy, and such commitments should last at least until the next market review. If commitments are insufficient, regulators should follow the ladder from the least intrusive remedy: passive access with transparency and non-discrimination first, then the EU harmonised access product, and active remedies only at last resort. Regulators should also take into account existing commercial agreements that already address market problems. Keeping the technology-neutral concept of “very high capacity network” instead of introducing “gigabit network”, as proposed by the Commission, favours regulatory predictability over changing the rules in the middle of the investment cycle.

Net neutrality that enables innovation. The Rapporteur defends the open internet principle and proposes only targeted measures to adapt the framework to technological progress. Traffic management measures should be able to differentiate between categories of traffic with different technical requirements, provided that all traffic within a category is treated equally. Specialised services should be offered only on a non-exclusive and non-discriminatory basis and where network capacity is sufficient, with legal certainty provided by a mandatory delegated act. This would open room for innovative, quality-assured services without weakening the open internet or consumer rights.

***2.2. Simplification***

The Rapporteur’s guiding idea is to reduce administrative and regulatory burden on undertakings in order to make space for growth and innovation.

General authorisation. The Rapporteur proposes that, instead of a separate notification in each Member State, providers should be able to use a single multilingual EU notification platform run by the ODN, which would automatically route notifications to the relevant national regulators. He supports the Commission’s proposals that no administrative decision should be needed before starting activity and that existing authorisations should remain valid. Notifications should be possible in any official language of the Union. Enforcement should be handled by the regulatory authorities in the Member States where services are provided.

Reporting. For the Rapporteur, no information that providers have already supplied under NIS2 or other Union law should be requested again. Data collection should follow the principle of data minimisation, sustainability data should be collected only where other Union law falls short, and BEREC’s data-collection guidelines should be reviewed every three years with a view to reducing the burden. Providers should supply information on open internet compliance at the regulator’s request rather than through the biennial reporting cycles proposed by the Commission. The Rapporteur also proposes to delete provisions whose benefits do not justify their cost: the spectrum geolocation database and pan-European numbering resources.

Practical bottlenecks should also be removed: grid connections for network sites should receive priority, and co-location assessments should rely on measured rather than theoretical electromagnetic emissions.

Copper switch-off. The Rapporteur proposes clear procedures instead of a single date for all. Member States where copper is already marginal (below 15% of fixed broadband subscribers) and which already have a binding switch-off plan approved by the national regulatory authority should be spared these rules and the related burden.

Governance and legal clarity. The Rapporteur proposes leaner governance and the use of the expertise of national regulators. The ODN should support BEREC instead of duplicating its work. Therefore, proposes that the key competences are performed by BEREC, while ODN would focus on providing administrative support to the regulatory work. ODN’s Management Board should be slimmed down from 27 to 9 members of the Board of Regulators, while 2 members appointed by the European Parliament should be added. New tasks for the ODN should come only with matching resources. For legal clarity, Member States should publish an implementation notice and repeal national provisions inconsistent with the Regulation.

***2.3. Harmonisation for the Single Market***

The draft report moves towards one market, at the level of infrastructure and at the level of services, so that European operators can scale and European consumers can benefit from services across borders.

Infrastructure. Beyond investment incentivising measures mentioned, the report proposes binding common EU methodologies for spectrum fees and reserve prices, a review of national award measures extended to coverage obligations and reserve prices, common authorisation conditions for wireless broadband in the Member States concerned, and the possibility of pan-EU awards. For access, the markets Recommendation should be reviewed at least every three years and contain common guidance on market definition and geographic differentiation. Access to satellite spectrum bands should be subject to general authorisation as a rule, and the conditions of the EU satellite authorisation, as well as the EU harmonised access product, should be laid down by delegated acts rather than implementing acts.

Services. The Rapporteur changes the Commission’s minimum harmonisation approach. He proposes stronger consumer protection rules at the EU level. They should include cost control, barring of third-party billing, warning before disconnection, comparison tools and quality-of-service parameters, together with a maximum commitment period of 24 months that applies everywhere, without national exceptions for any of those.

Roaming. Roaming is the single market at its best. The Rapporteur proposes to lower wholesale data caps from EUR 1.00/GB to EUR 0.65/GB, down to EUR 0.40/GB from 2031, and to extend the regime to 2037. Spectrum remains a scarce resource held by only a few mobile network operators in each Member State. Every other player therefore has to compete by using someone else’s network: domestic MVNOs through wholesale access agreements, and operators from other Member States through roaming agreements. Bringing wholesale roaming charges closer to what MVNOs pay for domestic wholesale access would create a level playing field between domestic MVNOs and operators from other Member States. Operators could then compete for customers across borders, and markets with high retail prices would come under pressure from offers made in other Member States. End-users would gain choice beyond their national market, while measures against anomalous or abusive use of wholesale roaming access are kept.

3. Resilience

Networks are critical infrastructure. The sabotage of undersea cables in the Baltic Sea, satellite navigation and radio jamming at the Union’s eastern border, large-scale power outages and extreme weather events in the south have shown that connectivity is a frontline of European security. The Rapporteur therefore makes network resilience a European task: knowing our vulnerabilities, closing them through investment, defending our spectrum and keeping people connected in every crisis. The draft report turns the Commission’s resilience provisions into a coherent framework built on six pillars.

***3.1. A resilience duty for the whole crisis-management chain***

The Rapporteur supports the Commission’s proposal for a duty to cooperate between providers, regulators and other authorities, and proposes to extend it to the authorities responsible for critical entities, so that the digital and physical resilience frameworks work together. He also supports the obligation on providers and emergency call centres to ensure uninterrupted critical communications, emergency communications and public warnings during disruptions and crises.

***3.2. A European Preparedness Plan focused on cross-border risks and dependencies***

BEREC should prepare the Union Preparedness Plan for Digital Infrastructures together with ENISA, the Commission, the NIS Cooperation Group and European crisis-response and resilience authorities. To deliver European added value rather than duplication, the Plan should complement national and Union plans on cybersecurity and crisis management. It should concentrate on the cross-border elements of network architecture, capacity and use, and regulators should not request again data already reported under NIS2.

The Rapporteur supports the Commission’s proposal that the Plan should provide a Union-level overview of network topology and identify route-diversification possibilities, bottlenecks and single points of failure, and that sensitive information should be handled under the rules on classified information and presented in aggregate form, so that critical assets cannot be geolocated. He also proposes a more realistic timetable for the Plan.

***3.3. From diagnosis to investment***

The Rapporteur proposes that the Plan’s recommendations should cover strategic cross-border connectivity investment needs. Member States and the Commission should take them into account and mobilise adequate resources, in particular under the next Multiannual Financial Framework, to strengthen network security and resilience. BEREC should share its analysis with the Commission, also on its own initiative, to guide Union funding, including for trans-European digital networks such as cable projects. Recognising submarine cables as electronic communications networks, giving priority to grid connections for network sites and densifying mobile networks would all add redundancy and strengthen resilience.

***3.4. Defending European airwaves and space assets***

The Rapporteur proposes a firmer response to cross-border interference and jamming. Operators in neighbouring countries should be able to agree directly on coordination. The Commission should be able to ask the Member State that licensed an interfering operator to take enforcement action, including fines and the withdrawal of the authorisation. Where interference comes from a third country, the Commission should be able to treat it as a matter of Union interest and negotiate on behalf of the Union. Joint assistance by Member States should follow after six months instead of 12 months, and the Council should be obliged to consider restrictive measures where the problem persists. Operators that suffer damage should have a right to seek compensation before national courts.

It should also be possible to refuse transfers or leases of spectrum rights where they pose risks to national security. In space, Member States should jointly preserve the strategic value of European ITU filings, act against speculative or abusive filings and be able to require their transfer. Transfers should require approval by the national filing authority and, where harmonised spectrum is involved, by the Commission. Satellite operators should also avoid harmful interference with satellite systems authorised in the Union or financed by Union programmes.

***3.5. Secure supply chains***

The Rapporteur supports the Commission’s proposal to make compliance with ICT supply-chain security requirements under the future Cybersecurity Act a condition of the general authorisation and of rights of use for spectrum, and proposes to extend it to the EU satellite authorisation. That authorisation is also conditional on data retention and lawful interception obligations, to which the Rapporteur adds the obligation to designate legal representatives. Enforcement should remain with national regulatory authorities under the general enforcement regime, which avoids a parallel procedure linked to legislation not yet adopted.

***3.6. Keeping people connected when technology changes or networks fail***

Where the switch-off of legacy technologies could affect emergency services or discontinue services on existing devices, providers should give three years’ notice instead of the two years proposed by the Commission, together with a migration roadmap. Regulators should assess the roadmap within six months, be able to impose mitigation measures and be explicitly tasked with supervising such migrations. Users should be informed if a migration affects their ability to call 112 with their current device.

Satellite providers should also ensure free access to 112, including in areas where terrestrial coverage is absent or critically disrupted, and contextual data and caller location should also be routed for calls made over satellite. Each national emergency system should designate a single IP gateway, and these gateways should be included in the Union database of emergency call centres so that centres can contact each other and exchange data across borders. Public warnings should be transmitted instantly, through one or several technologies.

4. Citizens

Last but not least, the Rapporteur wants a DNA that people feel in their daily lives: accessible to all, safer in emergencies and better at protecting them against fraud.

Accessibility. For persons with disabilities, total conversation, text relay and video relay services should be available and affordable, and emergency communications should include relay services available 24 hours a day. The 116000 hotline for missing children, public warnings sent through the European Digital Identity Wallet, and quality-of-service parameters should all take the needs of end-users with disabilities into account.

Emergency services and public warnings. The emergency number 112 should be reachable from all interpersonal communications services, including messaging applications, and not only where Member States so decide, as proposed by the Commission. It should also be reachable via satellite, free of charge, where terrestrial networks are unavailable. Emergency calls should carry automatically transmitted contextual data, not only location, and call-back from emergency centres should be ensured for as long as strictly necessary. Where public warning systems exist, warnings should reach affected users instantly, for example through cell broadcast, and Member States should be able to mandate several technologies for redundancy.

Roaming for frequent travellers. Under “roam like at home”, operators may apply a surcharge when a traveller’s data use exceeds the fair-use limit, and that surcharge is capped by the wholesale roaming charge. Lowering the wholesale caps, as the Rapporteur proposes, would therefore directly lower the maximum charges for those who use a lot of data abroad: frequent travellers, cross-border commuters, students and business travellers.

Fraud. The Rapporteur proposes to bring transit providers into the effort and to require all providers not only to identify but also to implement effective measures. BEREC guidelines should also cover information sharing to prevent, detect and mitigate fraud. To ensure that anti-fraud measures are effective, providers should be able to process certain traffic data, in line with the GDPR. The Rapporteur supports the Commission’s proposals on caller identification and on stopping automatic call forwarding by third parties, and extends the ban on hiding caller identification to the users of automated calling systems.

Fair and affordable services. The Rapporteur proposes that low-income consumers should receive support through vouchers, social allowances or direct payments. All consumers should get free tools to monitor consumption, alerts on unusual usage and warning before any disconnection. Contract termination fees should be reasonable and transparent, and consumers should be able to terminate free of charge where internet quality falls short of what was promised. Bills should be itemised by category, and complaint-handling and out-of-court dispute resolution should be strengthened, including for microenterprises and not-for-profit organisations.

II. Conclusion

With this draft report, the Rapporteur seeks to deliver a Digital Networks Act that matches the convergence of networks, computing and space: one that attracts investment, simplifies rules, completes the single market, strengthens Europe’s digital sovereignty and resilience, and brings tangible benefits to citizens.
